- **The two operators fold to an `i32` constant at compile time, and nothing downstream learns a new concept.** The type spelling folds in the parser (`primary()` already has the type and the array literal in hand); the expression spelling folds in `Sema::typeOf`, which is the one place that has the binding's dimensions. The folded value lands in `Expr::n`, so `gen` materialises it exactly like a `Num` and every constant path (`constantInteger`, `isConstExpr`, `foldConst`) reads it unchanged. Two `Expr::Kind`s (`SizeOf`, `Len`) and two fields (`ty`/`idims` for a type-form argument, `a` for an expression-form one) are the whole data-model change.
- **The real fix is that an array *parameter* now carries its length.** Before this round `fn f(a: i32[4])` decayed `a` to a bare pointer and threw the extent away (`Binding{pty, 0, …, {}}`), so `len(a)` inside `f` had nothing to answer from. `arrayParamType()` now hands back the dimension list it was already reading and the parser keeps it in `Function::paramDims`; `Sema::check` fills the binding with those dimensions (`len` = the product). This is what lets `len`/`sizeof` see a parameter at all, and it reuses the existing array queries — `arrayOperandOf` and a new `arrayElemCountOf` that returns the *grand total* rather than the outermost dimension. (C's own `sizeof` on such a parameter is still the pointer's 8; Aster answers the array's 16, which is what the caller passed — the C twin spells that out by measuring a real array type.)
- **It is new syntax only, so no program that compiled before changes by a byte.** The two identifiers are intercepted in `primary()` before the generic-name path; an existing program that never writes `sizeof` or `len` never reaches the new branches. Layer [8/9] asserts all **288** pre-existing baseline rows reproduce byte for byte, and `tests/rebuild-baseline-v0.23.35.sh` re-records with **306 → 309** rows (only `sizeof_len.ast` is new).
- **One honest refusal, in two spellings.** `len` of something with no element count is a compile error, not a silent answer: `len(x)` on a scalar, `len(p)` on a `ptr` (`len() requires an array, not a scalar or pointer`) and `len(i32)` / `len(P)` on a non-array *type* (`len() requires an array type, e.g. len(i32[4])`). `sizeof` of a scalar, a struct or a pointer is always well-defined, so it never refuses.
- **Coverage: `tests/sizeof-len-checks.sh` [9/9], registered in `ci.sh` beside `multi-dim-array-checks.sh`, with `examples/sizeof_len.ast` and its C twin `tests/sizeof-len-twin.c` (both exit 255).** (1) a strict `-Werror` rebuild; (2) **source guards** — the two kinds, the two fields, `dimsProduct`/`sizeofTypeValue`, the `primary()` intercept and its parse-time fold, the `len(i32)` refusal, `arrayParamType`'s out-parameter and its fill, `Function::paramDims`, the parse-loop collection, the `Binding{pty, plen, …, pd}` fill, `arrayElemCountOf`/`foldSizeOfExpr`, the `typeOf` fold, the two type-system `return Ty::I32` arms, the `gen` materialisation and the `constantInteger` fold; (3) **25 behavioural cases under qemu** — every spelling (type/expression) × every scope (type literal, local, parameter, global, struct instance, struct array field) × every width (i32/i64/u8/ptr), 1-D and 2-D, plus `len + sizeof` composition; (4) **3 gcc-differential pairs** — local and parameter arrays, struct and array-field scalar widths, and the shipped example against its twin; (5) **11 rejections** — `len` of a scalar/ptr/parameter-ptr/scalar-type/struct-type, four `sizeof` spelling errors (missing `(`, missing `)`, and a zero-length array type), and two contextual-keyword reads (`return len;` and `sizeof(len)` with `len` a local, both `expected '(' after len` — the names are not reserved, but nothing that reads them is anything but the operator); (6) a **25-row coverage floor**; (7) a **5-mutant layer** split across the three rule kinds (a parameter forgetting its length must now refuse, the expression-form not folding and the type-form ignoring its array literal must diverge, `len` reporting the outermost dimension must diverge, `len` of a scalar no longer refusing must now compile); (8) **306 of 306 pre-existing baseline rows byte-identical, 3 new** — `tests/rebuild-baseline-v0.23.35.sh` filters this round's `sizeof_len` rows, asserts the rest reproduce, and lands the total at 309; (9) the example's exit status.
- **One pre-existing guard moved its pin, and the full CI caught it.** `tests/operator-checks.sh` (the v0.23.17 round) pinned `Expr::Kind`'s tail as `…, BitNot, Cond } k;`; this round appends `SizeOf, Len` after `Cond`, so the guard now pins the kinds without the enum tail — the pair itself is guarded by `sizeof-len-checks.sh`'s own source-guard layer, so nothing is left unpinned. A full run's only red was that one guard: it is a test-only change, no compiler source moved.
- **What this deliberately does *not* do.** It does not make `sizeof` a **statement** or accept it without parentheses; it does not add a `typeof`, a `alignof`, or `sizeof` of a **function type**; and it does not change a **pointer's** `sizeof` away from 8 (the address is 8 bytes whatever it points at, which is the one place Aster and a decayed C parameter agree). The round also leaves the array-parameter **bounds check** absent, as it has been since v0.23.34-dev — a parameter is a pointer, and `sizeof`/`len` are the compile-time answers, not a runtime guard. That runtime guard is now closed by **v0.23.36-dev** (`--bounds-check` also traps an out-of-range parameter subscript).

## v0.23.34-dev — arrays as parameters and returns, strided `ptr<T>`, and a wired-up Lifecycle example

- **Where this came from.** The compiler could already hand an array to a function by reference (`&arr`, v0.23.30-dev) but only at four-byte stride, and could not take an array as a *parameter* at all — every array a function touched was a global for exactly that reason. The user was offered the next three items and answered *"把那三件一起补了"*: (1) arrays as parameters and return values, (2) pointers that carry their element's stride so `&i64[4]` / `&u8[4]` are no longer refused, and (3) the Lifecycle example that closes the README carry-forward about the `ANativeActivity` lifecycle callbacks.
- **Arrays as parameters decay to a typed pointer.** `fn sum(a: i32[4]) -> i32 { … a[i] … }` reads `a[i]` at the element's real width — `i32` at four bytes, `i64` at eight — because the parameter's element type is recorded once in `slotElemTypes` and read by both Sema (`typeOf`) and Codegen (`emitElemIndex`), so the two passes cannot disagree about the stride. A struct array parameter reaches its fields: `fn get(v: V3[2]) -> i32 { return v[1].a; }`. The array length is not part of the parameter type (a pointer carries no length), so there is no bounds check on a parameter — same as C (closed for `--bounds-check` in v0.23.36-dev).
- **`&arr` is no longer width-gated, and `ptr<T>` names the stride.** `Sema::arrayOperandOf()` in `typeOf(AddrOf)` now answers `tyPtrToElem(element)` for any element width, and `parseType` accepts `ptr<i64>` / `ptr<u8>` / `ptr<V3>` (the existing `ptr<S>` stays a struct pointer). A `ptr<T>` carries its element's byte width in `Ty::ew` plus the element's kind/sid, and `p[i]` strides at that width. `(&arr)[i]` indexes through the address directly — postfix now lets `[` follow an `&`, closing the chain with the named-array form. One honest guard: a strided pointer must not land in an **untyped** `ptr`, which would still stride four bytes and read the wrong element silently, so `let p: ptr = &w;` on an `i64[2]` is refused with a message that points at `ptr<i64>` or an array parameter; a `ptr<V3>` does not go into a `ptr<Q>` either.
- **Functions return a typed pointer.** `fn get(w: ptr<i64>) -> ptr<i64> { return w; }` round-trips a `ptr<i64>` through a call; `return a;` from an array parameter is still rejected as a dangling frame pointer. The `Ty` lattice gained three fields (`ew`/`ekind`/`esid`) and three free helpers (`tyPtrToElem`, `ptrElemType`, `tyIsPtrToElem`) because a pointer element cannot be a nested `Ty` (the element type is incomplete where the pointer is declared), so it is stored as kind+sid and reconstructed on use.
- **The Lifecycle example is wired and driven off-device.** `examples/lifecycle.ast` installs the seven `ANativeActivityCallbacks` lifecycle slots — `onStart`, `onResume`, `onPause`, `onStop`, `onDestroy`, `onConfigurationChanged`, `onLowMemory` — at offsets 0..48 (the window/input slots 56..96, which `native_render.ast` and `native_touch.ast` fill, are left alone), each bumping a counter that `aster_lifecycle_count(i)` reads back. `tests/lifecycle-callbacks.sh` builds it as an AArch64 `.so`, loads it under `qemu-aarch64` through a C host (`tests/lifecycle-harness.c`) with a host liblog stub (`tests/liblog-stub.c`) in place of liblog, calls `ANativeActivity_onCreate`, invokes each installed entry and asserts the counters — the same instructions Android runs, with only the loader (glibc, not bionic) and the log provider differing.
- **Coverage: `tests/array-address-checks.sh` [9/9] (updated) and `tests/lifecycle-callbacks.sh` [4/4] (new), both registered in `ci.sh`.** The array-address suite gained strided-pointer capability cases (u8/u16/i64 element widths through `&arr` and `ptr<T>`, a `ptr<V3>` reaching fields, a `ptr<i64>` returned and re-bound), a rejection matrix for the untyped-`ptr` stride guard, and an updated source guard plus the `[8/9]` filter for the new example; `tests/rebuild-baseline-v0.23.34.sh` asserts the 303 pre-existing baseline rows byte-identical and 3 new (the lifecycle example's three build modes). Two source guards elsewhere were re-aimed for the round's core edits (`postfix()`'s `[` after `&`, the `rejectBool`/`ptrToAssignable` call-site counts). No program that compiled before changes by a byte: **306 of 306** baseline rows reproduce, 0 regressions.

## v0.23.33-dev — `-g` grows a third section: `.eh_frame`, so a debugger can walk a stack

- **Where this came from.** It is the natural second half of the debug-information slice the roadmap had already scheduled as the line after `.debug_info`: *"The remaining debug-info work is call-frame information (`.eh_frame`), so a debugger can walk a stack rather than only print the frame it is in."* v0.23.32-dev made the frame base free (`DW_AT_frame_base = DW_OP_breg31 + 0`), which is exactly why this round is small: a debugger could already *read* every local, but it could not *unwind* — there was no `.eh_frame`. The round is again **not** a language change, so it is its own version, the rule v0.23.29-dev set when it split the four candidate rounds into one-theme versions. It reuses the patch-site / relocation mechanism v0.23.32-dev built, which is the whole reason it lands cleanly on all five consumers.
- **The core observation: a whole function's unwind table is six bytes.** Every Aster frame is a fixed **8192 bytes** (`SUB SP, SP, #8192`) and SP does not move again inside a body, so there is no frame pointer, no push/pop bookkeeping and no variable frame size — one CFA rule (from one instruction past the prologue, `CFA = r31 (sp) + 8192`) and, for a body that returns, one return-address rule (`r30` at `CFA − 8`). The entry, which *exits*, never stores `x30`, so its FDE has no such rule. The `preservesRa` flag (set opposite `Codegen::isMain`, and true for `main` in a shared object, where `main` is an ordinary returning function) is the only thing a function contributes to its FDE.
- **The CIE is copied from `aarch64-linux-gnu-gcc`'s real object, not the spec, and the suite checks it byte for byte.** Augmentation `zR`, code alignment 4, data alignment −8 (`SLEB128 0x78`), return-address column 30, augmentation data `0x1b` (`DW_EH_PE_pcrel | sdata4`), and the initial instruction `DW_CFA_def_cfa: r31, 0`. An FDE is `[length][cie_ptr][initial_location: pcrel sdata4][address_range: udata4][aug byte][cfi…][nop pad to 4]`; `initial_location` is a displacement from its own field, so a static image and a `.so` write it directly while an `ET_REL` object carries it as `R_AARCH64_PREL32` (261) against the function — confirming against gcc's own `.o` is what pins the layout.
- **The same patch-site idea serves the unwinder too.** `encodeEhFrame` writes each FDE's `initial_location` as a placeholder and records `(offset, symbol, addend)`; `writeObject` emits `R_AARCH64_PREL32` against the function, `writeElfBytes` and both `.so` writers resolve it from the segment addresses they compute, and `linkObjects` / `linkSharedObjects` rebuild one FDE per function with a `cie_ptr` back to the single merged CIE and an `initial_location` that points at the function's raised address. The `.eh_frame_hdr` is one sorted row per FDE (`function − header_vaddr`, `fde − header_vaddr`) under `PT_GNU_EH_FRAME`, and both sections sit in their **own `PT_LOAD`** because an unwinder reads them out of the running image — appending them to an existing load would move a boundary every other offset is computed from.
- **Four write paths and two linkers, all carrying the table.** `writeObject`, `writeElfBytes`, `writeSharedObject` (SysV-hash `.so`) and `writeSharedObjectDyn` (gnu-hash `.so`) each emit `.eh_frame` + `.eh_frame_hdr` + the two segments; `linkObjects` and `linkSharedObjects` merge them, one unit per input object, raised to that object's text address. Laid out after everything that already existed (and `SHF_ALLOC`, so the loader can find it), so **no pre-existing byte moves**: 303 of 303 baseline rows reproduce, with 0 new rows.
- **Coverage: `tests/eh-frame-checks.sh` [9/9], registered in `ci.sh` beside `debug-info-checks.sh`, with `tests/ehframe-decode.py` — a decoder written for this suite that reads the CIE/FDE bytes straight out of the ELF (no pyelftools, no objdump) and runs the CFI program.** (1) a strict `-Werror` rebuild; (2) **off by default** — no `.eh_frame`, no `.eh_frame_hdr`, no `PT_GNU_EH_FRAME` in any product without `-g` (checked across the 101 shipped examples too); (3) **additive** — `.text` compared byte for byte with and without the flag, and `.rela.eh_frame` carrying `R_AARCH64_PREL32` against every function; (4) **the CIE equals gcc's** — byte-for-byte against `aarch64-linux-gnu-gcc -O2 -c` for the same program; (5) **one FDE per function, agreeing with `.symtab`** — each `[begin, end)` equals its FUNC symbol's address and size; (6) **the CFI is tied to the code** — the CFA rule starts one instruction in (where `objdump` says `SUB SP, SP, #8192` ends) and `r30` is saved at `CFA − 8` by every returning body, never by the entry; (7) **the header is self-consistent and reachable** — `.eh_frame_hdr` names `.eh_frame`, carries one sorted row per FDE pointing at it, and both sections sit inside a `PT_LOAD` with a `PT_GNU_EH_FRAME` on the header; (8) **two independent readers agree** — `readelf --debug-dump=frames` and `llvm-dwarfdump --eh-frame` must describe the same functions and the same rules; (9) **it survives linking and running** — two objects produce one raised table, a shared object keeps every function's, and qemu says the images behave exactly as without `-g`; and a final mutation layer halves the CFA offset and drops the return-address rule and requires the suite to notice. Steps 4–6 are the ones that matter: they check the compiler against gcc, against the symbol table and against `objdump`, not against a number it produced.
- **What this deliberately does *not* do.** It does not emit **`DW_AT_call_frame_info`**, `DW_AT_call_file`/`DW_AT_call_line` on inlined work, location lists (an Aster local lives at one slot for its whole life) or a type DIE for a **function type**. It emits **DWARF v4, not v5**, and uses `SHF_ALLOC` `.eh_frame` rather than the `.eh_frame_hdr`-less compact form some linkers prefer. The byte baseline is not re-recorded: `tests/rebuild-baseline-v0.23.33.sh` asserts 303 pre-existing rows byte-identical and 0 new before it writes anything.

## v0.23.32-dev — `-g` grows a second section: DWARF v4 `.debug_info`

- **Where this came from.** The user was offered four candidate rounds at once — a struct array field, taking the address of a whole array, multi-dimensional arrays, and DWARF `.debug_info` — and answered *"这4项一起做"*. This is the fourth and last of them, and the only one that is **not** a language change: it adds nothing a program can write. It is therefore its own version rather than a slice of a language release, which is the rule v0.23.29-dev set when it split the four into three versions. The roadmap had carried it, in one line, since it scheduled the four: *"v0.23.32-dev | DWARF `.debug_info`: compilation unit, base types, function and variable DIEs (P4.2 follow-up)"* — and that line is also the scope, which is the useful part of writing it down in advance: the round emits exactly those four things and no more.
- **What was missing, in one sentence.** `-g` (v0.23.12-dev) let a debugger answer "which `.ast` line is this instruction?" and nothing else: the output held **no names, no types and no frame description**, so a local was an unnamed slot at an unnamed offset and a breakpoint by variable was impossible.
- **The interesting part: the frame base is free, and that is what makes the whole round small.** Every Aster frame is a fixed **8192 bytes** (`SUB SP, SP, #8192`) and SP does not move again inside a body, so `DW_AT_frame_base` is `DW_OP_breg31 + 0` — *SP itself* — and a variable's `DW_AT_location` is `DW_OP_fbreg(slot*8)`. No CFA rules, no `.eh_frame`, no return-address column: a debugger that can read SP can read every local. **Parameters ride the same rule**, because AAPCS64 arguments are spilled into slots 0..n-1 on entry (a struct taking `ceil(size/8)` of them), which is why a `DW_TAG_formal_parameter` is described exactly like a local and the encoder needs no separate notion of a parameter's home.
- **The second idea is that every address is *patched*, and each patch names a symbol.** The line table raised all of its addresses by one text base; `.debug_info` cannot, because a subprogram's `low_pc` is *that function's* address and a global's is *that object's*. So each address field is written as zero and recorded as `(offset, symbol, addend)` — `.text` for the unit, the function's own name for a subprogram, the object's for a global — and carried in **`.rela.debug_info`** as `R_AARCH64_ABS64` (257), the same relocation the line table uses, against a real symbol index. That is what makes one encoder serve five consumers: `writeObject` records the relocations, `linkObjects` and `linkSharedObjects` resolve them from the merged symbol map, and `writeSharedObject`/`writeSharedObjectDyn` resolve them from the segment addresses **they** compute. Nothing has to be told an address twice.
- **Types are emitted before anything that names them, and de-duplicated through one cache.** A `DW_FORM_ref4` is a *forward* offset — a DIE can only point at one that already exists — so `encodeDebugInfo` emits every type a function, a parameter, a local or a global will name before it emits the first variable, and `DwarfDieWriter::types` maps a type key to the DIE it already made. The keys make the C readings fall out: **an array is recursive** (`i32[2][3]` is two elements of `i32[3]`, not a flat six), a **`ptr<S>` reserves its reference and fills it after the recursive call** (a pointer may point at the struct it is a field of, whose DIE does not exist yet), and an untyped `ptr` points at a `DW_TAG_unspecified_type`.
- **Two constants were wrong the first time, and only an independent reader caught them.** `llvm-dwarfdump` rendered every type reference one DIE short — `i32[2]` where the source said `i32[2][3]` — because **a `DW_FORM_ref4` counts from the first byte of the unit *header*, not from the first DIE**, and the writer was handing out offsets into its own DIE buffer (the 11-byte header is prepended afterwards). And it printed `DW_TAG_partial_unit` where an unspecified type belonged: **`DW_TAG_unspecified_type` is `0x3b`**, and `0x3c` is the partial unit it was being confused with. Both are invisible to a decoder that trusts what it is given, which is the argument for `llvm-dwarfdump` being a witness in the suite and not just a debugging aid.
- **The linker's bug was the same class of mistake, and it is the one the suite was written to find.** Two `-g` objects linked to **two units whose `low_pc` were both the image's text base**: the second unit was raised to where *the image* starts rather than where *that object's* text landed, because the unit's patch names `.text` and the linker gives `.text` one address for the whole image. The line table never had this problem — it raises by an object-relative offset — so the fix is to carry the same thing: a `.text` patch's **addend becomes that object's offset in the merged text**, and the resolution `symbolVaddr[".text"] + addend` is then right for every object at once. `add2` sitting at `0x401030` while its unit claimed `0x401000` is the exact assertion that now pins it.
- **Four write paths, not one, and the two that were easy to forget are the ones that got wired last.** `writeObject` (an ET_REL) and `writeElfBytes` (a static image) came first; `writeSharedObjectDyn` (a gnu-hash `.so`) resolved its patches from the segment addresses it computes. But `build-so` has **two** paths — without a soname and without imports it takes `writeSharedObject`, the original SysV-hash writer — and `link-so` goes through `linkSharedObjects`, which is a *third* linker. Left alone, `-g` would have produced a `.so` with a line table and no `.debug_info`, which is the kind of inconsistency a user finds and a suite does not. All four now carry the sections, laid out after everything that already existed so that **no pre-existing byte moves**: 303 of 303 baseline rows reproduce, with 0 new rows (this round adds no example).
- **Coverage: `tests/debug-info-checks.sh` [9/9], registered in `ci.sh` beside `debugline-checks.sh`, with `tests/debuginfo-decode.py` — a decoder written for this suite that reads `.debug_abbrev` and `.debug_info` straight out of the ELF section table (no pyelftools, no objdump) and rebuilds the DIE tree.** (1) a strict `-Werror` rebuild; (2) **off by default** — no `.debug_info` and no `.debug_abbrev` in an executable, a PIE, an object or a shared object built without `-g`; (3) **additive** — `.text` compared byte for byte with and without the flag, and `.rela.debug_info` carrying `R_AARCH64_ABS64` against `.text`, a function and a global; (4) **the unit is anchored** — `low_pc`/`high_pc` read back from `readelf` rather than trusted, and `producer` naming the version the source says it is; (5) **one subprogram per function**, each `low_pc` and size equal to the values its own symbol has in the symbol table, its frame base `breg31+0`, its `decl_line` the line `fn` is written on; (6) **every variable reachable** — a local or parameter at an ascending 8-byte slot with the first at the frame base itself, on the source line it is declared on, with the type it was declared with; a global at `DW_OP_addr` equal to its symbol's address; (7) **the type DIEs are real** — a struct's byte size and each member's offset, an array's subrange, `i32[2][3]` spelled as an array *of* `i32[3]`, an 8-byte pointer, and no reference that points outside the unit; (8) **an independent reader agrees** — `llvm-dwarfdump` must produce the same functions and the same variables, DIE for DIE; (9) **the suite can still fail** — a copy of the source with every frame slot shifted by one must be caught. Steps 5 and 6 are the ones that matter: they check the compiler **against the symbol table and against the source**, not against a number the compiler also produced.
- **The suite's decoder found its own bug on the way, and it is the same bug as the linker's.** Reading the linked image, it printed `<bad-ref:70>` for every type in the second unit: a `DW_FORM_ref4` is relative to **the unit that holds it**, and the decoder was treating it as an offset into `.debug_info` — which is the same thing only for the first unit. `llvm-dwarfdump`, which parses unit by unit, resolved them correctly, and the disagreement is what identified which of the two was wrong.
- **What this deliberately does *not* do.** It does not emit **`DW_AT_call_frame_info`**, `DW_AT_call_file`/`DW_AT_decl_column` on inlined work, a `DW_TAG_lexical_block`, or a type DIE for a **function type** — `let fp: ptr = f;` still has no signature to print. It describes **a variable that moves** only as one slot for its whole life (no location lists) and nothing **optimized away** (there is no optimizer). It emits **DWARF v4, not v5**, and uses `DW_FORM_string` rather than a `.debug_str` section — one more section for a compiler whose debug output is a few hundred bytes. And it does not re-record the byte baseline: `tests/rebuild-baseline-v0.23.32.sh` asserts **303 pre-existing rows byte-identical and 0 new** before it writes anything. (The `.eh_frame` it deliberately left out — SP *is* the frame base, so a debugger could print locals without it, but could not *walk* a stack — is exactly what **v0.23.33-dev** adds.)

## v0.23.31-dev — a second subscript: `T[N][M]`, where `a[i]` is a row and `a[i][j]` the element

- **Where this came from.** The user was offered four candidate rounds at once — a struct array field, taking the address of a whole array, multi-dimensional arrays, and DWARF `.debug_info` — and answered *"这4项一起做"*. This is the third of them, and the one the roadmap kept rescheduling: v0.23.29-dev and v0.23.30-dev both carried `T[N][M]` in their plans, and v0.23.30-dev split it out again when building `&arr` showed the two share no code. It is the item two previous rounds each named as out of scope — v0.23.28-dev ended with *"no multi-dimensional array"* and v0.23.29-dev with *"a nested array field is multi-dimensional arrays, the next language round"* — so the language's arrays have been one-dimensional by design since v0.22.49-dev and this is the round that changes that, exactly once.
- **What was missing, in one sentence.** A subscript was the last thing you could do to an array: `a[i]` *was* the element, and that was the end of the expression, so `a[i][j]` was a syntax error and a grid had to be flattened by hand (`a[i*3+j]`, with the 3 written out and re-derived at every use).
- **The interesting part: it is the same decay one level in, and no new type carries it.** In C, `a[i]` on an `int[2][3]` is a *row* — the address of three ints — and `a[i][j]` is the element. Aster already has exactly that shape: an array name decays to a `ptr` (v0.23.30-dev made that address reachable by name and by `&`), so `a` is a `ptr`, `a[i]` is a `ptr`, and `a[i][j]` is `i32`. The round therefore adds **no `Ty::Kind`**, no array type in the lattice and no pointer-with-a-stride: the type system does not move at all. What changes is only *which number each subscript multiplies by*.
- **The stride rule is the whole feature.** A subscript at dimension *k* strides by `dims[k+1] * … * dims[L-1] * sizeof(elem)`. For the innermost subscript that is `sizeof(elem)` — the one stride a one-dimensional array has always had, which is why no pre-existing byte moves — and for an outer one it is the product of the dimensions below it: **12** for a row of a 2×3 `i32` grid, **24** for a row of a 2×3 `i64` one, **4** for a row of a 2×3 `u8` one. `count` and `len` stay the *total* element count and the outer length respectively, so the frame reservation, the broadcast initializer, `.data`/`.bss` sizing and `layoutStruct` are all untouched; the dimensions travel beside them in a new `dims` vector (a local `Binding`, the global `arrayDims` map, a `StructField`, and codegen's `slotDims`).
- **Five places moved together, and none of them is a new mechanism.** (1) **`arrayDimList()` reads one `[N]` per dimension** at every declaration site — a local, a global in all three branches, a struct field — while `dimsTotal()` bounds the total the way the one-dimensional spelling always did, so `i32[1000][1000]` is still refused by the same 1..1000 / 1..1048576 rule and `i32[0][3]` is refused where the length is read. (2) **One chain shape** serves assignment and expression alike: the array's name rides on the innermost `Index` node and each subscript above it is another `Index` whose `a` is the chain below and whose `b` is this subscript — which is why `a[i][j] = v` and `a[i][j]` reach the *same* address computation, and a single subscript still comes back as itself, leaving the one-dimensional path unchanged in every respect. (3) **Sema walks the chain** (`indexChainOf`): dimension *k*'s bounds come from `dims[k]`, the answer is a row unless this is the innermost subscript, and *every* subscript in the chain is checked. (4) **Codegen computes every subscript first and materializes the base address after** in `emitIndexAddr()`, because evaluating a subscript can materialize a global's address into X16 — which is exactly where the base is about to go — and then scales each subscript by `emitElemStride` (a shift for a power-of-two stride, a 32-bit materialize plus `UMADLL` otherwise, v0.23.28-dev's rule reused). (5) **A chain that stops short of the elements addresses a row, and a store to a row is refused**, in both assignment forms — including the single-subscript spelling, which arrives from the parser as itself and is told apart from a chain by the subscript count the parser records (`Stmt::subs`).
- **The round found four holes in its own first draft, and each one is a rejection-matrix row now.** `a[1] = 5` on a two-dimensional array was **accepted** — a single subscript comes back from the parser as a bare expression, so the row test that looks at the chain's shape never saw it, and the fix is the parser-recorded count (`s.subs < 2` is a one-dimensional store and must not be judged as a row). `a[true][0]` was accepted: the first version of the chain walk checked only the *outermost* subscript, so an inner `bool` literal rode through the hole the per-level loop exists to close. `x[0][1]` on a **scalar** reported "pointer indexing is not supported yet" instead of "cannot index non-array variable: x", because the ptr-chain check was placed before the inner subscript's own type error. And a one-dimensional read regressed to the *unnamed* bounds message (`array index out of bounds:` without the array's name), because the chain path took over the last remaining `checkArrayBounds(uint32_t, …)` call — the level-0 named base now calls the named overload again. All four were found by the suite, not by reading, which is the argument for the suite.
- **Coverage: `tests/multi-dim-array-checks.sh` [9/9], registered in `ci.sh` after `array-address-checks.sh`.** (1) a strict `-Werror` rebuild; (2) **source guards** — `arrayDimList`'s leading `[` consumption, four `dimsTotal` sites, `indexSubscripts` carrying the count, the interleaved `Dot || LBracket` postfix loop admitting a bracket above a bracket, `IndexChain`/`indexChainOf`/`indexChainIsRow`/`arrayDimsOfName`, the per-level loop, `Binding`'s `dims`, `emitIndexAddr`/`emitElemStride`/`slotDims`/`globalArrayDims`, the `levels = depth + 1` walk, the short-chain stride line, the row-store gate, `unsigned subs = 1;`, and the empty-name ptr-chain check; (3) **12 behavioural cases under qemu** — a 2-D write/read, computed subscripts on both levels, a row handed to a function (local and 3-D), a global grid, a struct field, an `i64` grid at stride 24, a `u8` grid at stride 1, a row as a `ptr` value, a compound assign through the chain, and chains inside two nested `for` loops; (4) **4 gcc-differential pairs** — 2-D and 3-D with rows to a function, `i64`/`u8`/field grids, a global grid written through a row, and `examples/multi_dim_array.ast` against its C twin `tests/multi-dim-array-twin.c`, both exiting **255**; (5) **22 rejections** — row stores in four spellings (single subscript, compound, 3-D, field) plus a `for` step, a row into an `i32` and into an `i32` parameter, two levels on a `ptr`, a bool subscript at either level, three bounds messages, a zero dimension, a too-large total, three bases that are not arrays (scalar, unknown, 1-D global/field in the too-many-subscripts spelling), and a row in a condition; (6) a **36-row coverage map**; (7) an **8-mutant layer**; (8) **288/288 pre-existing baseline rows byte-identical, 3 new**; (9) the example's exit status.
- **The mutants split three ways, because the rules are of three kinds.** Withdrawing a *refusal* (the row store, the per-level check) must **now compile** — the mutant accepts what the real compiler refuses, and the rejection matrix above is what pins the refusal. Withdrawing an *acceptance path* (the chain builder, the leading `[`, the local and global dimension registration) must **not compile** — a program that used to build now does not. And damaging *arithmetic* must **diverge**: m1 drops the short-chain stride so a row is one element wide, witnessed by a row-sum of 15 that becomes 9; m2 drops the outer stride multiply, and its witness deliberately reads `a[0][2]` rather than the element it just wrote — a read back through the same broken chain cancels the damage, so the witness lands on an element the broken *fill* had written last (3 real, 5 broken).
- **Ten resident suites were updated on the record, and the first full CI run is what found the second five.** The five that were known while the round was being built: `array-bounds-checks.sh` pinned the 1-D message and stays green only because the named overload is back on the level-0 path; `array-elem-types-checks.sh` refused `struct P { x: i32[2][2] }` — **legal now**, since a field may be multi-dimensional — and its m5/m10/m11/m12 mutants aimed at spellings this round rewrote (the element-type answer now comes from the chain branch, and the length arithmetic is spelled `dimsTotal`); `struct-array-field-checks.sh`'s `StructField` guard and its m4 both pointed at the pre-`dims` spellings; and `precedence-checks.sh` and `array-address-checks.sh` each carry the byte-baseline filter, which gained this round's family. **The full CI run then surfaced five more of exactly the kind the history has recorded before** (v0.23.24-dev, v0.23.28-dev): count guards whose subject this round legitimately grew — `condition-checks.sh` counted 17 `rejectBool(` occurrences and now counts 18, the 17th call being the per-level chain walk; `wide-int-literal-checks.sh` counted 8 SXTW leaves and now counts 9, the ninth being the chain read's own leaf, the twin of the one-dimensional statement two arms below it; `local-array-checks.sh` and `for-loop-checks.sh` each counted four `ADD Xd, SP, #slot*8` frame bases with `sh=0` and now count five, the fifth being `emitIndexAddr`'s — the chain path materializes the same local base, after its subscripts, which is the whole X16 ordering rule; and `sema-checks.sh` pinned `struct Binding { … }` and now pins the field it gained. Every count was raised to the number the source has, with the new site named at the guard — none was weakened, and the mechanical re-evaluation of the remaining count guards in the tree found no sixth casualty.
- **What this deliberately does *not* do.** It does not make an array **a parameter or a return value** — a row is a `ptr`, exactly the value `&buf` produced last round, and it travels the same way. It does not let a **row be stored to** (`a[1] = 5` is refused, because a store to a row is as meaningless as a store to the array itself) or used as a **condition** (a row is a ptr, and a ptr is not a truth value). It does not let a chain **run past the innermost dimension** (`a[0][1]` on an `i32[3]` is refused: `too many subscripts for 'a': it has 1 dimension`). It does not add a **pointer type that carries a stride**, a **`sizeof`**, or a way to ask an array for its length in an expression — so an `i64[N][M]`'s row still cannot be handed to a `ptr` parameter with a wrong stride; it is refused or used as a value, as before. And it does not re-record the byte baseline: `tests/rebuild-baseline-v0.23.31.sh` asserts 300 pre-existing rows before it writes 303.

## v0.23.30-dev — an array has an address: `&buf` is a `ptr`

- **Where this came from.** The user was offered four candidate rounds at once — a struct array field, taking the address of a whole array, multi-dimensional arrays, and DWARF `.debug_info` — and answered *"这4项一起做"*. The roadmap's first rule is that a version is **one coherent theme**, so the four were split into three versions, and this is the second of them. The roadmap had scheduled `&arr` **together with** `T[N][M]` in v0.23.30-dev, on the grounds that both are "an array is a thing with an address and a shape". Building it showed that they share no code at all: `&arr` is one branch in `Sema::typeOf` over an address that is already being computed, while `T[N][M]` is a recursive length carried through the parser (several `[N]` pairs), through sema (a length vector) and through codegen (nested scaling). Two changes with no line in common are two versions, so `T[N][M]` moves to **v0.23.31-dev** and the debug-info slice to **v0.23.32-dev**.
- **What was missing, in one sentence.** `&` got past its lvalue gate and then had exactly one branch: the operand had to be a struct, or it threw `'&' is only defined for a struct lvalue, not …`. An array was therefore the one thing in the language a function could not be handed — unless it lived at file scope, which is why every array a function touches in the shipped corpus is a global.
- **The interesting part: the address was already there.** A local `T[N]`'s `Binding.ty` *is* `Ty::Ptr` — the array has been decayed at declaration — and `gen(Var)` already emits `ADD Xd, SP, #slot*8` for it, which is what "an array name decays to its address in an expression" has always meant in bytes. A global `T[N]`'s `typeOf` answers `Ty::Ptr` too and `emitGlobalAddress` materializes it. So this round has **no parser change, no new instruction and no new encoding**: nothing had to learn to compute an address that was not already being computed. What was missing is only the rule that lets `&` ask for it.
- **So "is this an array?" is a question about the length, not the type.** Because a named array is decayed, an `i32[4]` and a `ptr` are the *same type* — `Ty::Ptr` — and no amount of looking at `Binding.ty` can tell them apart. `Sema::arrayOperandOf()` therefore answers by **length**, in three arms: a local (`b->len`), a file-scope array (`arrayGlobals` + `arrayElemTypeOf`/`arrayLengthOf`), and a struct's array field (`arrayFieldOf()`, the v0.23.29-dev free function, whose `StructField::len` is the same "has a length" test in a third place).
- **One new rule, and it is the honest one.** `p[i]` on a `ptr` has always been `*(p + i*4)`, and Aster has no pointer type that carries a stride — so handing over the address of an `i64[4]` would be silently lying about the distance between elements. The gate is `if (tySize(aelem) != 4u) throw …`, and the diagnostic names the element type and says why: `'&' on an array needs a 4-byte element type: this array's elements are i64, and a ptr indexes at 4 bytes`. That admits `i32[N]`, `u32[N]`, `bool[N]` (a 4-byte 0/1 slot an element) and an `S[N]` whose `sizeof(S)` is 4, and refuses `i64`, `u8`, `u16`, `ptr` and a 12-byte struct. Refusing is better than scaling wrongly, and the alternative — a fatter pointer — is a type-system change, not a one-line gate.
- **`&arr` answers a plain `ptr`, not a `ptr<T>`.** The address of an array is the address of its first element, and `ptr` is the type that can be indexed, stored and passed. A `ptr<S>` would also have brought struct-field syntax (`p.f`) to something whose fields are elements, which is not a thing the language has. The struct branch below it is untouched and still answers `ptr<S>`.
- **Coverage: `tests/array-address-checks.sh` [9/9], registered in `ci.sh` after `struct-array-field-checks.sh`.** (1) a strict `-Werror` rebuild; (2) **source guards** — `arrayOperandOf`'s definition and its three arms, the `if(arrayOperandOf(*e.a,aelem,alen))` call, the `tySize(aelem)!=4u` gate and its message, plus the two pre-existing `&` rules pinned again so a round that rewrites the `AddrOf` arm cannot quietly drop them; (3) **12 behavioural cases under qemu** — a local read through a reference, a store through one that the caller must see, a global, a `bool[3]`, a `u32[4]`, a 4-byte struct element, `&s.cells` behind a `ptr<S>`, the address kept in a variable and indexed at a computed offset, two arrays proving two slots do not alias, a reference handed to a second callee, and `&buf == buf` (the address equals the decayed name, which is the round's whole premise); (4) **3 gcc-differential pairs** — the three shapes, the field shapes, and `examples/array_address.ast` against its C twin `tests/array-address-twin.c`, both exiting **63**; (5) **14 rejections** — the 4-byte gate in all three shapes and for five element types, `&buf` into an `i32` parameter, and six rules that must **not** have moved (`&x` on a scalar, a ptr, a bool, a scalar field, a call result, and `&a[0]`); (6) an **18-row coverage map**; (7) a **7-mutant layer**; (8) **288/288 pre-existing baseline rows byte-identical, 3 new**; (9) the example's exit status.
- **Two mutants are worth naming.** **m2** makes the local arm answer the *decayed* type (`b->ty`, 8 bytes) instead of the element type — which is the mistake the round's central insight exists to prevent, and which shows up as a refusal of a perfectly ordinary `i32[4]`: it is a `must_not_compile`, because a mutant that cannot compile is a refusal and not a red battery. **m7** drops the `*8` from the frame address, so `&a` names the wrong place for every array past slot 0 — the exact shape of the v0.22.50-dev frame-base bug, with the same kind of invisibility (an array in slot 0 is unaffected), which is why its witness declares a `pad` local first and reads 7.
- **What this deliberately does *not* do.** It does not make an array **a parameter or a return value** — `&arr` hands the address over, and the callee still sees a `ptr`. It does not add a **pointer type that carries a stride**, so an array whose element is not 4 bytes wide still cannot be handed over at all (that is the gate above, not a bug). It does not let **`&a[0]`** through — an element is an lvalue but `&` is still defined for a struct element alone, so `&a[0]` on an `i32` array is refused exactly as it was. It does not add **multi-dimensional arrays** (`T[N][M]`, now v0.23.31-dev), **`sizeof`**, or a way to ask an array for its length in an expression. And it does not re-record the byte baseline: `tests/rebuild-baseline-v0.23.30.sh` asserts 297 pre-existing rows before it writes 300.

## v0.23.29-dev — a struct field may be an array: `xs: i32[4]`

- **Where this came from.** The user asked for four candidate rounds at once — a struct array field, taking the address of an array, multi-dimensional arrays, and DWARF `.debug_info` — and answered *"这4项一起做"*. The roadmap's first rule is that a version is **one coherent topic**, so the four are not one release: the three language features are this round and the next one (v0.23.30-dev: the address of a whole array, and `T[N][M]`), and the debug-info work is a toolchain round of its own after that (v0.23.31-dev). This round is the first of the three, and it is the one the previous round named as out of scope: v0.23.28-dev ended with *"a struct field is still not an array"*.
- **What was missing, in one sentence each.** `structDeclaration()` parsed `field: T` and stopped, so `nums: i32[4]` was a syntax error (`expected ',' or '}'`). The field type had nowhere to keep a length, so even parsing it would not have made it addressable. `postfix()` ran a `.` loop and then a `[` loop, two separate passes, so `s.pts[1].a` — a bracket *between* two dots — could not be built at all. And a struct's size was the sum of `tySize(field)` over its fields, with nothing to multiply by.
- **The data model is one field: `StructField::len`.** `layoutStruct()` lays an array field out at `sizeof(element) * len` bytes and records the offset; every later read, store and address computation reads `len` back from the field it named. A length is bounded to **1..1000**, the same range a local array takes, and it is checked where it is parsed (`array field length must be between 1 and 1000`). Named arrays and array fields stay two different paths on purpose: a named `Index` carries the array's name in `e.name` and its index in `e.a`, while a field `Index` carries the `Field` in `e.a`, the index in `e.b` and an empty `name` — so `arrayFieldOf()` can tell them apart without a new node kind.
- **Brackets and dots are one loop now.** `postfix()` interleaves them — `while (cur().k == TK::Dot || cur().k == TK::LBracket)` — which is what makes `s.pts[1].a`, `s.xs[i].f = v` and `&s.pts[1]` parse. The old two-loop shape could only ever have produced a dot chain followed by a bracket chain, and the first thing every real user of an array field writes is a bracket *followed by* a dot.
- **One new address emitter, and it is made of two that already existed.** `emitElemAddrField()` is `emitStructAddr()` (the field's base, whether the struct is a local, a global or behind a pointer) composed with `emitElemIndex()` (the index scaled by `elemShiftOf`, a shift for a power of two and `UMADDL` otherwise — v0.23.28-dev's rule, reused unchanged). So a 12-byte struct element inside a struct is scaled by a multiply for the same reason a 12-byte element of a named array is, and there is no second copy of that arithmetic to disagree.
- **Bounds checking split into two overloads by what it knows.** A named array is checked by *name* (the compiler looks the binding up); a field is checked by *length*, because the length is right there on the `StructField` and there is no binding to look up. `checkArrayBounds(uint32_t length, …)` and `emitBoundsCheck` acquired length forms next to the name forms they already had.
- **A struct that contains an array field cannot be built by a literal — and that is a rule, not a gap.** `S { … }` fills each field from one value, and an array field needs `N` of them; the language has no aggregate-of-repeated-values syntax, so the honest answer is to refuse at both scopes (`struct 'B' contains an array field and cannot be initialized by a literal`) and give the user the shape that does work: **declare without an initializer and write the elements**. `structHasArrayField()` is recursive, so a struct *containing* a struct with an array field is refused too. A struct local that has no initializer is now zero-filled — which is new in its own right, since until now every `let` had to have one.
- **The zero-fill was wrong the first time, and wrong in a way only a `ptr` field could see.** An uninitialized struct local emitted `STR X0, [SP, #imm]` where it meant `STR XZR, [SP, #imm]`: the zero-fill encoding had no `Rt` at all, and `Rt == 0` is `x0`, not XZR (`31`). Every uninitialized struct local was therefore filled with whatever `x0` happened to hold. The shipped example had survived this only by luck — the statement before it left `x0` at zero. The `ptr field element` battery case, which writes an address into `b.ptrs[1]` and then reads `b.ptrs[0]`, was the first witness that could see it; the encoding is now `0xf90003e0u | 31u | ((slot + i) << 10)` and a source guard pins the `31u`.
- **Two other defects surfaced on the way, both from the no-initializer shape.** `emitStructAddr()`'s `Index` arm threw `cannot take the address of this expression` for any non-struct element, which is a *Sema* rule about `&` that had been copied into codegen — it blocked the ordinary store path `b.ptrs[1] = &anchor`, not just `&b.ptrs[1]`. The throw is gone; the `AddrOf` rule in Sema still owns that boundary. And `Sema::checkStmt()`'s non-array `Let` branch dereferenced `*s.expr` unconditionally, so `let b: Buffers;` — legal now — segfaulted inside `typeOf()`; the branch begins with `if (!s.expr) { …; return; }`.
- **Coverage: `tests/struct-array-field-checks.sh` [9/9], registered in `ci.sh` after `relro-checks.sh`.** (1) a strict `-Werror` rebuild; (2) **source guards** — `StructField::len`, the 1..1000 message, `arrayFieldOf`, `emitElemAddrField`, `structHasArrayField` and the literal refusals, the interleaved `Dot || LBracket` loop, both `checkArrayBounds`/`emitBoundsCheck` length overloads, and the `31u` in the zero-fill encoding; (3) **8 behavioural cases under qemu** — `i32` (local and global), `bool` (as a condition), `ptr` (null fill, then an address), a 12-byte `struct` element, `&b.pts[1]` → `ptr<V3>` with a deref, an element written in both scopes, and a **whole struct element copied** into a struct variable; (4) **2 gcc-differential pairs**, one covering every element type in both scopes and one being `examples/struct_array_field.ast` against `tests/struct-array-field-twin.c` (both exit **63**); (5) **10 rejections** — indexing a scalar, struct or bool field, `s.x[0].y`, a constant out-of-bounds index, both literal initializers, both length bounds, and a struct element read into an `i32`; (6) a **14-row coverage map**; (7) a **6-mutant layer**; (8) **288/288 pre-existing baseline rows byte-identical**; (9) the example's exit status.
- **Three mutants had to be re-aimed, and each one is a lesson about what a witness can see.** **m4** (dropping the field length so the element stride collapses) had to become a `must_not_compile` — with the length gone the source no longer builds, which is a refusal, not a red battery. **m5** (scaling every element by 4 instead of by `sizeof(element)`) was first witnessed by reading back the element it had just written, which is exactly the one read that cannot see a wrong stride; the witness now writes `pts[1].a` and reads `pts[0].b`, a different element, where the wrong scale lands on a zero. **m6** (storing `x0` instead of XZR) is the bug above, re-created deliberately; the source guard on `31u` makes the obvious `sed` useless, so the mutant rewrites the encoding whole and the witness is the `ptr` case.
- **What this deliberately does *not* do.** It does not let a struct field be **a nested array** (`xs: i32[4][4]` — that is multi-dimensional arrays, the next language round). It does not let an array field be **initialized by a literal**, or a **whole array field be assigned** (`b.nums = …`). It does not let an array field be **a parameter or a return value**, or a struct containing one be **passed to C** as an aggregate — a struct with an array field is still copied by value under AAPCS64, but only Aster programs declare one. It does not add a **`sizeof`** or a way to ask a struct for its size. And it does not re-record the byte baseline: `tests/rebuild-baseline-v0.23.29.sh` asserts 294 pre-existing rows before it writes 297.

## v0.23.28-dev — an array's element type is the type written before the `[N]`

- **Where this came from.** The user asked what the basic types are and whether anything still needs filling in: *"现在编译器基本类型有哪些，需要扩充完善没？"* The honest answer came in two halves. The **types themselves are complete** — `i32` `u32` `i64` `u8` `u16` `ptr` `bool` and a struct, each with a size, an alignment and a set of rules — so the answer to "do the types need扩充" is no. What was incomplete is what a type means **inside an array**: `T[N]` was only ever `T = an integer`. Offered as three holes and one rule, with the lowest-risk item first, the user picked it: *"补齐数组元素类型（推荐）"*.
- **Hole (1): a local array's element type was gated on being an integer.** `letStatement()` opened the `[N]` suffix only when the type was one of the integer kinds, so a local `bool[4]`, `Point[4]` or `ptr[4]` was not a declaration Aster could parse at all — and a local `ptr[4]` carried a refusal of its own ("local pointer arrays are not supported"). The gate is gone: the element type is whatever stands before the `[`, and every later read, store and address computation reads it back from there.
- **Hole (2): neither of the two closed global branches looked for a `[`.** `globalDeclaration()` has three branches — Bool, Struct, and the scalar one — and only the third parsed a length. `let flags: bool[4] = true;` and `let pts: Point[4] = Point { … };` were syntax errors while `let xs: i32[4] = 0;` two dozen lines below them was fine. Both branches now parse `[N]` with the same 1..1048576 check, and `layoutObject()` repeats a struct's constant image once per element, which is what "every element is a copy" means in bytes.
- **Hole (3): the type checker answered `i32` for every subscript, whatever the element was.** `Sema::typeOf(Expr::Index)` was a hardcoded `Ty::I32`, while codegen loaded and stored at the element's real width — so the two halves of the compiler disagreed, and the disagreement was visible from the language: `let q: Point = a[1];` was a type error against itself. `Sema::Binding` carries the element type next to the length, `arrayElemTypeOf()` reads it back, and `typeOf(Index)` answers it.
- **The one rule underneath all three: a struct element is scaled by a multiply, not by a shift.** A struct's size is a multiple of its alignment and need not be a power of two — `{ i32, i32, i32 }` is 12 — so the old `tyShift`, which answered 2 for every type it did not name, could not scale a struct element at all. `elemShiftOf()` answers the log2 when the size is a power of two and **-1** when it is not, and `emitElemIndex()` falls back to `UMADDL X16, Wsize, Windex, X16`. For every scalar the two agree, which is why no pre-existing element address moves.
- **The fallback's first spelling was wrong, and it was wrong in the one way AArch64 punishes quietly.** The natural way to widen the index is `ADD Xt, XZR, Widx, UXTW #0` — but in the *extended* add `Rn == 31` is the **stack pointer**, not XZR, so every struct array read added SP and walked off the frame (a 12-byte element's read segfaulted before it was fixed). `UMADDL` reads both operands as the 32-bit values they are and needs no zero-extension of its own. The trap is recorded in a comment next to the emit, and a source guard asserts the comment is still there.
- **One address, not four.** Every path that reaches an element — a read, a store, a field of one, `&a[i]` — called its own copy of "materialize the base, scale the index, bounds-check". `emitElemAddr()` is now the single place that does it, over three bases (a local `T[N]` at its frame slot, a fixed-size global by `ADRP`+`ADD`, and a `ptr` local at the one `i32` stride it has always used). They cannot disagree about the base, the scale or the bounds check any more — which is the actual reason hole (3) was reachable.
- **`a[i].f = v` needed a dispatch of its own.** The token that distinguishes it from `a[i] = v` is the one *after* the closing bracket, and the subscript is an arbitrary expression that may contain another bracket (`p[i[0]].x`), so `indexedThenField()` matches brackets by depth before it decides. `isLValue` now admits an element, so `&a[i]` and a whole-element copy are addressable, and `emitStructAddr` reaches an element when — and only when — the element really is a struct.
- **The boundaries are the element's own rules, not the array's.** A bool element takes `true`/`false` or the 0/1 constant and nothing else (a new `boolArrayValue` reads the literal no integer folder can); a ptr element takes the null constant or another address; an integer element refuses both; a struct has no register form, so a whole element is still not a value. A **struct field is still not an array** (`array fields are not supported`), a ptr *local* still indexes at the `i32` stride rather than becoming an array of pointers, and the two length limits are unchanged.
- **No program that compiled before changes by a byte.** Every shipped example that indexes an array declares it with an integer element type, and `elemShiftOf` agrees with the old `tyShift` for every scalar — so the change is argued before it is measured, and then measured: **288 of 288 pre-existing rows reproduce byte for byte**, the three new rows are `examples/array_elem_types.ast`'s three output modes, and `tests/rebuild-baseline-v0.23.28.sh` refuses to write the baseline unless both hold (291 → 294 lines).
- **Coverage: `tests/array-elem-types-checks.sh` [9/9], registered in `ci.sh` after `precedence-checks.sh`.** (1) a strict `-Werror` rebuild; (2) **source guards** — the local extension and its missing gate, `Binding.elem` and `arrayElemTypeOf`, all three global `[N]` parsers counted at 3, the layout repetition, `elemShiftOf`'s -1 and the absence of the old `tyShift`, `emitElemIndex`/`emitElemAddr`, the `UMADDL` encoding, the XZR/SP comment, `isLValue`, `emitStructAddr`'s element arm, the condition walker's third form, `indexedThenField` and its dispatch, `boolArrayValue`, both `initializerFits` bool arms and the ptr address fill; (3) **24 behavioural cases under qemu** — bool/ptr/struct elements in both scopes, an 8-byte struct, a 12-byte one, a nested subscript in a field target, `&a[i]`, a whole-element copy and a whole-element parameter, plus the four widths that already worked (`i64` `u8` `u16` `i32`) and the ptr-local stride that must not become one; (4) **6 gcc-differential pairs**, including the example against `tests/array-elem-types-example-twin.c` (both exit 127); (5) **28 rejections**; (6) a **30-row coverage map**; (7) a **15-mutant layer**; (8) **288/288 of the A/B baseline**; (9) the example's exit status.
- **Two mutants earned their shape the hard way.** **m2** (a 12-byte element scaled by 1) was first pinned to `t[2].a = 5; return t[0].a` and survived: at one byte per element index 2 lands *two bytes into* `t[0].a`, and the only byte a process exit status carries is the low one — so the store was invisible. The witness moved to index 4, whose wrong address is `t[0].b`'s first byte (5, against the real 0). **m6** (an element is not an lvalue) was first pinned to `a[i].f = v` and survived too: `isLValue` is what `&` asks, and the field store runs through a different gate — so the witness is `&a[i]`, refused one layer above m7's codegen twin.
- **Eleven resident suites were updated on the record, not quietly broken.** `condition-checks.sh` pinned `genBool`'s two delegations and now pins three (an element is a condition); `for-loop-checks.sh` and `local-array-checks.sh` counted three `ADD Xd, SP, #slot*8` frame bases and now count four, the fourth being the shared element-address emitter; `precedence-checks.sh` counted two copies of the local array range message and now counts three, because sema's array branch answers the element type and a bool array carries the check too; `runtime-bounds-checks.sh` pinned the read path's `emitBoundsCheck(e.name,*e.a,r)` and now pins the two calls inside `emitElemAddr`; `sema-checks.sh` pinned `Binding` and now pins the field it gained; and `pointer-index-checks.sh` pinned the blanket refusal of a ptr store through `p[i]`, which has narrowed into the element-type rule (`p[0]` on a ptr still names an `i32`) — the refusal is unchanged, the message is specific now. The full CI run then surfaced four more of
  the same kind, where a guard's *subject* became legal or a site became shared: `local-array-checks.sh`
  refused `let p: ptr[2] = 0;` — legal now — and refuses `a[0] = true` on a local `i32[2]` instead;
  `wide-int-checks.sh` pinned `tyShift` and pins `elemShiftOf` now (the same numbers for every scalar,
  **-1** for a size that is not a power of two); `wide-int-literal-checks.sh` counted three index SXTW
  leaves and nine sites and counts one shared leaf — caught three times over, 54/57/58 — and seven
  sites, with `slotElemTypes` written in three places, `M6a`/`M6b` merged into `M6`, and `M11` re-aimed
  from the ptr leaf to the element's **load width** (81/82/87); `condition-checks.sh` refuses
  `array init = true` as a *type* mismatch rather than a non-constant initializer, and re-aims mutants
  **m4** and **m6** because both rules gained a twin this round (m4: the fold chokepoint is defence in
  depth now, so it withdraws the 0/1 constant a bool slot accepts; m6: the global gate and its
  `initializerFits` twin).
- **What this deliberately does *not* do.** It does not allow a **struct field to be an array** (`P.f[4]` is still refused — that is a different feature with its own layout questions). It does not give a **`ptr` local an element type** — `p[i]` still means `*(p + i*4)`, because making a pointer's stride depend on a declared type is a language-design question, not a gap. It does not let a **whole struct element be a value** (it has no register form; copy it field by field, or take `&a[i]`), or a **`ptr[N]` element be compared for ordering**, or an array be **a parameter or a return value** — unchanged. It does not add a **`sizeof`**, a **multi-dimensional array**, or **array assignment as a whole**. And it does not re-record the byte baseline: `tests/rebuild-baseline-v0.23.28.sh` asserts 291 pre-existing rows before it writes 294.

## v0.23.27-dev — four places Aster read an expression differently from C, closed

- **Where this came from.** The previous round was a document: `docs/language-reference.md`, written out of the source and then checked line by line against the binary. Its §9 recorded four divergences from C, found by *testing* and not by reading, and two of them were named as candidate rounds rather than design. The user's answer to the list was one line — *"好的，全部开始修"* — so all four closed in this round.
- **The four, and what each one was.** (1) **`&&` and `||` shared one precedence level**: one loop collected both, so `a || b && c` was read `(a || b) && c` — a different answer, silently. (2) **`-` `!` `~` `*` took a `primary()` operand**, so `-s.v` parsed as `(-s).v` and `*s.p` as `(*s).p`; in practice that made all four *rejected* whenever the operand was a struct, since `(-s)` is one. (3) **`a < b == c` was rejected** by the anti-chaining check, although the comment directly above `relational()` had always promised `(a < b) == c` — a comment/implementation mismatch, not a decision. (4) **A local `let` skipped the `initializerFits` range check a global `let` has always applied**, so `let b: u8 = 300;` stored 44 inside a function while the same line at file scope was an error.
- **(1) and (2) are grammar, and both are one new level.** `logicalAnd()` now collects `&&` over `equality()` and `logical()` collects `||` over that, so `&&` is the tighter one, as in C and as `docs/language-spec.md` had been claiming for releases. A new `unary()` level sits between `postfix()` and the multiplicative level and holds all five prefix operators — `&` moved onto it from `postfix()`, where it had been the odd one out and already behaved correctly. Both dereference-assignment targets (`*p = …`, `*p += …`) parse their target at `unary()` depth, so `*p.f = v` now means what C means.
- **(3) is a one-predicate narrowing that makes the comment true.** `isComparisonOp` split into `isRelationalOp` (`< <= > >=`) and `isEqualityOp` (`== !=`); each level now refuses a chain *within itself* and says nothing about the other. `a < b < c` and `a == b == c` are still refused — both levels are non-associating, so `(a < b) < c` would compare a Bool against an integer — while `a < b == c` reads `(a < b) == c` and `a == b < c` reads `a == (b < c)`.
- **(4) is the only one that needed a decision, and the decision is narrow.** A local `let` now runs `initializerFits` — for a scalar *and* for an array's element initializer — in sema and again in codegen, with the same message in both passes. **Only a constant initializer is checked**: narrowing a *value* (`let b: i32 = <an i64>;`, `let b: u8 = <an i32>;`) is still accepted, as it has always been, because that is a conversion the language offers rather than a constant that does not fit. `let b: u8 = 300;` is an error now; `let b: u8 = <an i32 holding 200>;` is not.
- **No program that compiled before changes by a byte, and the round carries no codegen at all.** Every one of the four is a parser or type rule, so the evidence is the A/B baseline: **288 of 288 pre-existing rows reproduce byte for byte**, and the only new rows are `examples/precedence.ast`'s three output modes (291 total). The zero-impact claim was also argued before it was measured — no shipped example mixes `&&` with `||`, none writes `*x.y` or `-x.y`, `a < b == c` did not compile at all, and none of the 411 `let` declarations in `examples/` and `tests/` initializes a narrow local out of range.
- **Coverage: `tests/precedence-checks.sh` [9/9], registered in `ci.sh` after `pointer-index-checks.sh`.** (1) a strict `-Werror` rebuild; (2) **source guards** — `logicalAnd()` exists and loops `&&`, `logical()` loops `||` over it, the old one-operator loop is gone from the source, `unary()` exists and all five prefixes take a `unary()` operand, `product()` starts there, both dereference-assignment targets parse there, `isRelationalOp`/`isEqualityOp` exist and `isComparisonOp` does not, and all four range checks plus both of their messages are counted (each message must appear twice, once per pass); (3) **33 behavioural cases under qemu**; (4) **5 gcc-differential pairs**, including `examples/precedence.ast` against its C twin `tests/precedence-example-twin.c` (both return 127); (5) **16 rejections** — six comparison chains, seven out-of-range locals, and three type rules the new `unary()` depth does not loosen (`-b` on a bool, `!` on an i32, `&` on an i32); (6) a **33-row coverage map** naming which rule each case is there for; (7) a **12-mutant layer**; (8) **288/288 of the A/B baseline**; (9) the example's exit status.
- **Three mutants earned their shape the hard way, and each one taught something about what "covered" means.** **m2** (the two levels swapped) was first pinned to a row whose answer the *grouping* decides — and the mutant still gave the right answer, because `&&` and `||` agree on that row under both groupings. It needed a row where the swapped *nodes* disagree as well: `T && F || F`, false under `(T && F) || F` and true under `T && (F || F)`. **m7** (equality() stops refusing a chain) showed that some rules are *diagnostics*, not gates: withdrawing it does not admit `a == b == c`, it just moves the failure one token later to "expected ')'", so what the mutant loses is the specific message — and that is what the rejection-matrix row is there to notice. **m8/m10** withdraw the range check from *one* pass only and must still be refused by the other, which is what proves the codegen twin is a live gate rather than dead code copied from sema; m9/m11 withdraw both and must be accepted.
- **What this deliberately does *not* do.** It does not accept a comparison **chain within one level** (`a < b < c` is still an error, and so is `a == b == c` — write `a < b && b < c`). It does not make a narrow local's **value narrowing** an error (only an out-of-range *constant* is). It does not add a `null` keyword, change `?:`, or touch the comparison fast path's codegen. And it does not re-record the byte baseline: `tests/rebuild-baseline-v0.23.27.sh` asserts 288 pre-existing rows before it writes 291.

## v0.23.26-dev (docs) — `docs/language-reference.md`

- **Where this came from.** The user asked for it in one line: *"现在编译器语法、关键字、保留字等编译器内容给我"*. The project had `docs/language-spec.md`, which argues feature by feature and explains *why*; it had no lookup table for *what*. The two are different documents and only one of them existed.
- **Nothing in the compiler changed.** No source edit, no test, no baseline row. The round is a document, and the version string stays `0.23.26-dev` because the behaviour it describes is v0.23.26-dev's.
- **Written from the source, not from memory.** The 21 reserved words come from the keyword chain in `Lexer::run()`; the operator set from its `switch`; precedence from the `primary()` → `postfix()` → `product()` → … → `expr()` ladder; the quotas from the literal limits scattered through `Parser`.
- **Every rule was then run against the binary.** All 21 keywords were tested one at a time as `let <kw>: i32 = 1;` and all 21 are rejected. Precedence was checked by execution (`1 + 2 * 3` = 7, `1 << 3 + 1` = 16, `a + b << c` = 12). Rejections quote the compiler's own message, copied from the output rather than paraphrased.
- **The four divergences from C in §9 were found by testing, not by reading.** (1) `&&` and `||` share one precedence level — `1==1 || 1==1 && 1==2` evaluates to false, where C would say true. (2) `-` `!` `~` `*` take a `primary()` operand, not a `postfix()` one, so `-s.v` reads as `(-s).v` and is rejected for a struct; `&` is the exception and takes a `postfix()`. (3) `a < b == c` is rejected by the anti-chaining check even though the comment above `relational()` says it parses as `(a < b) == c` — a comment/implementation mismatch. (4) A local `let` skips the `initializerFits` range check a global applies, so `let b: u8 = 300;` truncates locally and is rejected globally.
- **(3) and (4) are candidate rounds, not documented intent.** (3) is a one-line fix if the intent in the comment is the intent; (4) needs a decision about whether narrowing a local is a warning, an error, or allowed. Both are recorded in the reference rather than quietly fixed, because fixing either changes what existing programs compile.

## v0.23.26-dev — a pointer can finally be tested: the null constant and `==`/`!=` for `ptr`

- **Where this came from.** The user asked the question directly after the conditions round: *"null和非null可以判断吗"*. The honest answer was no, and in three ways at once — which is what made it a worthwhile round rather than a one-line patch.
- **The gap had three halves, and all three had to close together.** (1) **`==` was refused for a `ptr`**: three copies of `pointer comparison is not supported yet` (sema's `typeOf`, and codegen's `genBool` and `emitCondition`). (2) **There was no null to compare against**: `let p: ptr = 0;`, `f(0)` and `return 0;` from a `-> ptr` function were all type errors. (3) The conditions round had just refused `if (p)` on purpose, so truthiness was not the way out either. A round that fixed only the comparison would have shipped `p == 0` with no way to produce a null except a zero global in `.bss`.
- **The feature is one idea: the literal `0` in a pointer context is null.** `isNullConstant` — `Num`, not a bool literal, value 0 — is admitted by four gates (a local, an assignment, an argument, a return) in both passes, plus the two general assignment rules (`tyAssignable` on the value lattice and sema's `ptrToAssignable`, which gained the `Expr` it needed to tell `0` from any other integer). `==`/`!=` then accept a ptr against another ptr or against `0`, in either operand order.
- **The bool-literal trap is the reason the predicate looks the way it does.** `false` is a `Num` whose value is also `0`, so a predicate written as `e.n==0` would let a bool be an address. `isNullConstant` tests `boolLit` for exactly that, and mutant m3 (deleting the test) must make `let p: ptr = false;` compile — the mutation exists to prove the refusal is live.
- **No new codegen.** `tyIs64(Ty)` already returned true for `Ty::Ptr`, so the comparison path's existing width rule (`wide = tyIs64(ta)||tyIs64(tb)`) was already choosing `CMP Xn, Xm` — confirmed in the disassembly before a line of type rule was changed, which is why the round carries no encoding risk and why the byte baseline holds by construction.
- **Two boundaries are deliberate.** **Ordering two addresses stays rejected** — which of two unrelated objects "came first" is not a question the language answers — and **`if (p)` stays rejected**, keeping v0.23.25-dev's rule that a ptr is an address, not a truth value. Only the literal `0` crosses the integer/ptr line: `p == 1`, `p == -1`, `p == x` (an i32 variable), `let p: ptr = 1` and `let p: ptr = false` are all still errors.
- **Coverage: `tests/null-ptr-checks.sh` [9/9], registered in `ci.sh` after `condition-checks.sh`.** (1) a strict `-Werror` rebuild; (2) **source guards** — the predicate, `tyAssignable`, `ptrToAssignable`'s new signature and all 4 of its sites, the eight gates, both new messages (the ordering one counted at 3: sema plus both codegen twins), and an assertion that the old blanket refusal is gone from the source; (3) **19 behavioural cases under qemu** — the constant in every position it may appear, both equality directions (`p == 0` and `0 == p`), a comparison stored as a bool and composed with `&&`/`!`, the search shape (hit and miss), a pointer read out of memory with `load_ptr`, `ptr<S>` null and non-null, a loop driven by `p != 0`, a global null and an array name's address; (4) **5 gcc-differential cases** — the search shape, two names for one object, null through a parameter and a return, a null test stored as a value, and the shipped example against its twin; (5) **26 rejections**; (6) a 22-row coverage map; (7) an **8-mutant layer**; (8) **288/288 of the A/B baseline reproduces** — 285 pre-existing rows byte-identical; (9) the shipped example's exit status.
- **Two of the mutants earned their shape the hard way.** m2 was first aimed at `genBool`, but a condition runs through `emitCondition` (whose operand is named `c`), so `if (p == 0)` compiled unchanged — the mutant was split into m2 (conditions) and m2c (a comparison used as a *bool value*), proving both twins are live. m4 — allowing `p < q` — cannot be produced by damaging one pass, because the other still refuses: the round's rule lives in sema *and* in codegen, and the mutant records that both halves must be withdrawn, which is the point of having two.
- **Three test cases were my own mistakes, not the compiler's.** `0 == p` was written as a rejection when it is the same test as `p == 0` (it moved to the behavioural battery). `0 + p` was written as a rejection when pointer arithmetic has always been legal (replaced by a struct-against-0 case). And the mutation that drops the null-constant guard had `if(true)` where it needed `if(false)` — the guard reads `if (!null) throw`, so dropping it means making the condition unreachable. Each was found by the suite refusing to pass on a premise that was false.
- **One resident suite was updated on the record.** `sema-checks.sh` pinned `if (a > 0)` producing `pointer comparison is not supported yet`; that message no longer exists, having narrowed into two answers (ordering, and an integer that is not 0). The case now asserts the ordering message and a second case asserts the integer one — the boundary the old blanket refusal covered is still covered, just named precisely.
- **Byte-level: 285 of 285 pre-existing outputs reproduce byte for byte** — asserted by `struct-checks.sh` [4/8] — and the 3 new rows are `examples/null_ptr.ast`'s three output modes. `tests/rebuild-baseline-v0.23.26.sh` refuses to write the baseline unless every pre-existing row reproduces.
- **What this deliberately does *not* do.** It does not add a **`null` keyword** (the literal `0` is the spelling, as in C, and a keyword would be a second way to say the same thing). It does not make a **`ptr` truthy** — `if (p)` is still refused. It does not define **ordering** for addresses, or a ptr's **arithmetic** relationship to any integer but the offset it already had. It does not let a **non-zero integer** become an address, nor a **`bool`**, nor a **`ptr` become an integer** — every one of those refusals is in the matrix.

## v0.23.25-dev — conditions speak truth: `true`/`false` literals and integer truthiness in `if`/`while`/`for`

- **Where this came from.** The user named the round directly: *"下一步完善if、while条件表达式bool类型true/false，0和非0"*. Aster's conditions accepted exactly one form — a comparison — so `if (x)` with `x: bool` was a compile error, and a `bool` value could only be manufactured by a comparison operator because **the language had no `bool` literal at all**: `Expr::Num` was born carrying an `i32` and nothing else.
- **The language surface is two halves of one idea.** (1) `true`/`false` become keywords and `primary()` builds a `Num` carrying a `boolLit` flag, so the literal's *type* is `Bool` while its stored value stays 0/1 — the two `typeOf` twins (sema and codegen) are the only places a literal's type is decided, and both now answer `Ty::Bool` for the flag. (2) The condition machine — `checkCondition` (sema) and `emitCondition` (codegen), already shared by `if`/`while`/`for` — accepts a comparison (the pre-existing `CMP`/branch fast path, untouched so the byte baseline holds by construction), a `bool` expression (measured against zero, exactly as a comparison's answer already was), or **any integer, 0 = false, non-zero = true**.
- **The width rule is the round's one real codegen subtlety.** An integer condition must be tested against zero *at its own width*: `i32`/`u8`/`u16`/`u32` use the comparison path's existing `CMP Wn,#0`, but an `i64` needs the new `CMP Xn,#0` (`cmpZeroX`) — a 32-bit compare of an `i64` holding a value past four billion reads as zero, which is precisely the bug a 32-bit-only zero test would ship. The mutation layer pins this with a case that must diverge (1 → 0).
- **The boundary is deliberate: `?:`, `&&`, `||` and `!` stay strictly `bool`.** Truthiness coercion lives in control-flow conditions and nowhere else, so `a && b` still means "both true", not "both non-zero", and `if (a && b)` works because the `&&` answers a `bool` that the condition accepts — two layers, one clean. `ptr` and struct conditions remain rejected; only integers and `bool` are truthy types.
- **Promoting the literal exposed four real gaps, all filled in the same round.** (1) `genBool` had never handled `Field` or `Cond`, so `if (s.b)` and `c ? a : b` died in codegen with "expected a bool expression" although sema had already typed them `bool` — a pre-existing defect the literal work made impossible to ignore; both now delegate to `gen(e, Ty::Bool)`. (2) Arithmetic silently accepted `bool` operands through the integer-widening paths (`1 + true`, `~true`, `-true`, `a[true]` all compiled); one `rejectBool` guard now sits at all fifteen operator sites (negation, the five binary arithmetics, three shifts, four bitwise ops, bitwise not) and array indexing answers "cannot use a bool value in an array index". (3) `constantInteger` folded `true` to `1`, which let `let x: i32 = true` and `a[true]` compile; a `bool` literal is no longer a constant integer. (4) Global and struct-field initializers had no type gate for the new literal — `let n: i32 = true;` and a `bool` into a non-`bool` field are now positioned errors naming both types.
- **Dead code was removed on sight, not shipped untestable.** An `isBoolConstant` branch for `boolLit` is unreachable by construction: the function is consulted only when the actual type is *not* `Bool`, and a `boolLit`'s type always is. The draft was withdrawn rather than left as a branch no test can reach.
